#2937. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2933. Should You Buy Nvidia Stock Now That Jensen Huang Has Resigned? Here’s What I’m Doing.

Nvidia has gone through a rough few weeks.

Nvidia (NVDA -2.01%) has had a tumultuous few days. News broke that CEO Jensen Huang had an affair with someone who is an alleged Chinese spy. That's a major problem considering how vehemently the U.S. government has fought to keep Nvidia's cutting-edge technology out of China's hands.

This has caused Nvidia’s stock to sell off over the past few days, compounding the fact that it was already declining before this story broke. The reality is that the broader stock market believes AI spending has gone too far and is no longer willing to fully value companies in this sector.

With some other relationships coming to light and Huang resigning on Nov. 12, Nvidia is entering a new paradigm.

Those are two negative forces working against Nvidia stock, which is why it's down 30% from its all-time high. The question is, should you sit this one out or buy more? Here's what I'm doing.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. Image source: Getty Images.

The biggest question investors must ask themselves is whether anything has changed inside Nvidia's business. I think the answer to that is no.

The AI build-out is still going strong, and several AI hyperscalers have already indicated that 2027's build-out costs will be greater than in 2026. In fact, Nvidia gave guidance that it expects data center capital expenditures by the big five AI hyperscalers to reach $1.3 trillion in 2027, up from nearly $800 billion in 2026. This is projected to lead to 70% revenue growth next year, which is far faster than the market is currently pricing into the stock.

The biggest question is what Nvidia will look like without Huang. If the board can identify a suitable long-term replacement, then there's nothing to worry about. However, if the company faces some government-led investigations about what's going on with its business in China through intermediaries, this could be a huge problem for Nvidia.

Furthermore, future product development could be an issue, as Huang is viewed as a visionary in his field. There are still many key pieces within the Nvidia organization that can keep the ship on course.

I still think the company is in a great position to dominate regardless of who is in the leadership position, but not anyone can run Nvidia. While CFO Colette Kress is taking over as interim CEO, investors should be wary only if she's named the full-time replacement. CEOs with finance backgrounds have harmed many tech companies; just look at what happened with Intel and IBM. If Nvidia's board doesn't learn that lesson, its future could be in jeopardy.

However, I have faith that the board will make the right decision. With the most recent sell-off, now is also one of the best times in recent memory to buy the stock.

After the sell-off, Nvidia trades for just 21 times earnings.

That's significant because it now prices Nvidia at a cheaper level than the S&P 500, which trades at 22.4 times earnings. That's an amazing stat, especially considering that Nvidia's growth is expected to be 70% next year.

When next year's earnings projections are used to price the stock, it trades for just 10.6 times next year's earnings. That's just an absurdly low price tag for a company positioned to capitalize on the AI build-out, regardless of who leads it.

As a result, I think Nvidia is still a strong buy today. With how cheap the stock has gotten due to the sell-off, there are few better options available in the stock market than Nvidia. While it may take a bit for the stock to regain its swagger, I think it will come with time. There have been many companies that have recovered from failures or scandals, and I believe that Nvidia will be among them in the next few months.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 823%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 209% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

That's the six degrees of separation. Every fellow human on Earth is six degrees away from every other. Here's my way of explaining that.

Your friend is obviously influencing you. That is degree No. 1. Your friend's friend is also influencing you, and your friend might say, Hey, my buddy this or that said this or that thing or is doing this new thing. You hear that your friend's friend. While many humanistic concepts go back to the Greeks, the phrase Self-Actualization only showed up in the 20th century, most famously popularized by American psychologist Abraham Maslow. He put it at the top of the pyramid of his so-called Hierarchy of Needs. If we can get there, we have gone beyond merely fulfilling our biological and egotistical needs to our psychological ones; we have, in effect, maximized our potential. Now, you may already be familiar with Maslow's hierarchy, but do you know the wisdom of Positive Intelligence genius, Shirzad Chamine? Shirzad says we approach this in the wrong order. Oh my God, we think, I have to figure out what my big achievement will be on this earth. Then we must focus and achieve it. Having achieved it, well, I'll be self actualized. Wrong.

Each of us, with no map or guide, is standing at the edge of a dark forest. There is a lighthouse in the distance, self actualization. Getting to the lighthouse is a goal taken one step at a time. At any given step, Chamine urges, Take the step that has more light in it. Chamine urges, Take the step that has more light in it. Only after you take that step, will the next be revealed? Light symbolizes positive emotions we're drawn to like empathy, curiosity, creativity, fulfillment. Their opposites are fear, anxiety, self doubt. With several choices in front of you, ask, which step has more light? Take that step, and as a consequence, there'll be more light on your path, and the next step will be revealed. As you keep taking each new step toward the light, things will get brighter, and you will discover tools you didn't know about, new friends you now recognize as crucial. Every step is full of discovery and growth, Shirzad says, and you keep getting closer and closer to the lighthouse.

Yet, surprise ending, you will never make it to the lighthouse. Because nobody is ever fully self-actualized. Every step you take, you are becoming more and more your true self. The value lies in the journey to becoming your authentic, unique, best self. That's The Lighthouse. As we close out this Mailbag, let me just say this one had a really nice generational dimension. We've got a 13-year-old, a 19-year-old, a 50-year-old thinking about time, freedom, and a retiree navigating required minimum distributions because of a too-large snowball that kept making money rolling down the hill. #2924. AGNC Sold About $1 Billion of New Stock Last Quarter at $10 a Share. Is Today's Lower Price a Better Deal?

The mREIT continues to dilute its investors as its book value shrinks.

AGNC (AGNC +0.37%), the world's second-largest mortgage real estate investment trust (mREIT), has increased its share count by 8,310% since its 2008 IPO. That dilution isn't surprising, since mREITs constantly issue new shares to fund their ongoing investments.

However, that strategy works better when there's enough demand to drive its stock higher and offset that dilution. In the third quarter, AGNC raised $992 million by selling 97.6 million shares at an average price of $10.17 per share. But today, its stock trades at about $8. Does that discount -- and its forward dividend yield of 17.5% -- actually make its stock worth buying?

AGNC usually borrows at short-term rates to buy longer-term mortgage-backed securities (MBS). This business model churns out stable profits when interest rates are low, but it struggles when rising rates boost its short-term borrowing costs and erode the value of its older, lower-yielding MBS. That's why the Fed's 11 consecutive rate hikes in 2022 and 2023 severely reduced its book value per share and net spread and dollar roll income per share, which is a more accurate gauge of an mREIT's profitability than its earnings per share (EPS).

From 2021 to 2025, AGNC's book value per share plunged from $15.75 to $8.88, and sank to $7.13 per share in the third quarter of 2026. Its net spread and dollar roll income per share declined from $3.11 in 2021 to $1.50 in 2025.

The Fed held rates steady in 2024 and 2025, but AGNC's book value and profits didn't immediately bounce back because it takes time to roll off its older MBS from its balance sheet. Its prospects would have brightened this year if the Fed started cutting rates again, but the Iran war and persistent inflation drove it to raise interest rates again this September. That pressure is making AGNC an unappealing investment, even though it pays a double-digit dividend yield.

AGNC launched an updated $1 billion buyback plan (about 10% of its market cap) in the third quarter, but it will only start buying back its shares if they drop below its book value. That hasn't happened yet, and its book value could still shrink faster than its stock price.

For now, analysts expect its net spread and dollar roll income per share to dip from $1.54 in 2026 to $1.49 by 2028, but it can still cover its forward dividend rate of $1.44 per share. At $8 per share, it might look like a bargain at five times this year's net spread and dollar roll income, but it's not worth buying until interest rates decline and the macro environment improves.

#2920. Where Will Intuitive Surgical Be in 10 Years? Hint: Its da Vinci 5 Installed Base Just Topped 2,000

Intuitive Surgical is struggling. Its newest da Vinci system is.

Intuitive Surgical (ISRG +0.84%) is having a terrible year. The company's shares are down 21% to date, as of writing, amid somewhat mixed financial results. Also, over the past 18 months, the company has faced margin pressure, partly due to steeper tariffs. That said, there are some reasons to be excited about Intuitive Surgical's future. One of them is the da Vinci 5, the latest version of its famous robotic-assisted surgery (RAS) device.

The da Vinci 5 has become popular since it earned regulatory clearance in 2024, and it recently exceeded an installed base of 2,000 devices. Could this device help Intuitive Surgical bounce back and perform well over the next decade?

According to Intuitive Surgical, the da Vinci 5 introduced more than 150 enhancements over its predecessor. Some of the most notable include significantly more computing power, improved imaging to give surgeons better visualization of anatomical structures, and Force Feedback Technology, which allows physicians to better gauge the force they are exerting on patient tissue and adjust accordingly, potentially reducing tissue damage during procedures.

These and other features have made the da Vinci 5 a hit. In the third quarter, Intuitive Surgical placed 546 systems, an increase of 28% year over year.

Da Vinci 5 systems accounted for 315 total placements, compared to 240 in the year-ago period. Intuitive Surgical now has a da Vinci system installed base of 12,094, up 12% year over year -- and more than 2,000 of them are of the latest da Vinci 5. Intuitive Surgical first launched its famous robotic surgery device in 2000. Yet, just about two years after launch, the latest version of it already accounts for over 16.5% of its total installed base. That's impressive.

Over the next 10 years, we should see rising demand for robotic surgery. Here are two reasons why. First, the market is currently underpenetrated. According to some estimates, fewer than 8% of eligible soft tissue procedures are performed robotically. This number should rise in the long run, since the minimally invasive procedures enabled by robotic devices offer significant advantages, such as smaller incisions, reduced bleeding and scarring, and shorter hospital stays.

Second, the world's population will age over the next 10 years. Since older people are more likely to need many of the surgeries for which the da Vinci system is indicated, this could also be a secular tailwind for the RAS market. Intuitive Surgical is already the industry leader, but the da Vinci 5 could help the medical device company extend its lead.

The device's features, particularly the Force Feedback Technology, could improve surgeons' adoption and lead to higher procedure volume -- and more instrument and accessory sales -- than the healthcare leader would have otherwise. Intuitive Surgical is also looking to make the da Vinci 5 the cornerstone of its strategy within a market it has yet to enter: Telesurgery.

Surgeons' ability to perform surgeries remotely with the da Vinci 5 could boost Intuitive Surgical's revenue by expanding access to robotic surgery, encouraging more hospitals to purchase the da Vinci system, increasing procedure volume and instrument and accessory sales, and perhaps creating new revenue streams from software and connectivity services.

Management has targeted the first half of 2028 for telesurgery clearance. Now, Intuitive Surgical could remain somewhat volatile in the short run as it navigates recent headwinds. However, the company boasts a massive addressable market that could enable it to bounce back and perform well over the long run.

And after the beating the stock took this year, now may be a great time for investors to initiate a position. Holding onto the stock for the next decade could lead to excellent returns.

#2917. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2904. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2893. Ecuador wants to monetize its biodiversity — while still drilling for oil

Ecuador, known for its "megadiverse" Galapagos Islands, Amazon rainforest, Andes and Pacific coast, wants to protect its natural landscapes for years to come — but it faces a tricky balancing act.

As well as preserving its nature, the South American country also plans to capitalize on its critical minerals and continue to extract oil from parts of the Amazon — both of which have led to protests from Indigenous and environmental groups.

One way Ecuador is planning to channel funds into its nature is via new financial tools, such as "debt-for-nature" swaps and its first sovereign green bond. It is also aiming to raise its environmental profile on the global stage by hosting the U.N.'s biodiversity summit, known as COP18, in 2028.

Ecuador, which uses the U.S. dollar, is no stranger to green finance. In March, IDB Invest, the private-sector arm of the Inter-American Development Bank, and Ecuadorian lender Banco Bolivariano issued a $120 million biodiversity bond to back conservation projects. The government also completed two swaps in 2023 and 2024 — deals in which a country's debt is refinanced on better terms in exchange for commitments to spend the savings on conservation. Together, they freed up about $1.1 billion for conservation in the Amazon and Galapagos Islands.

Juan Carlos Blum, Ecuador's minister of environment and energy, said the country hopes to issue sovereign green bonds for the first time in 2027, to raise finance to protect its coastal and mountainous regions and the people whose livelihoods depend on them. The government is looking to the European Union for advice on such instruments, he said. The bloc set green bond standards in 2024.

For Blum, one of the challenges is public awareness. "A lot of us are not aware of how we depend on nature, how we depend on the biodiversity, how vulnerable we all are towards climate," he told CNBC by video call.

Another challenge for Ecuador, as it looks to protect its biodiversity, is its reliance on oil.

The country is a significant oil producer in Central and South America and in October, the government announced plans to open up new tenders for oil exploration in parts of the Amazon, according to local media reports. The news was reportedly met with criticism from Indigenous leaders, who strongly oppose the plans.

"We are being careful on that balance between biodiversity and natural resources use, renewables or not renewables, and it's a delicate matter," Blum said. "That's my day-to-day reality, dealing with balancing this."

About 80% of Ecuador's energy was generated from oil and oil products in 2024, according to the International Energy Agency, while about two-thirds of its electricity was produced using hydroelectric power.

Cracking down on illegal mining is a focus for Blum, who told CNBC he was aware of the environmental impact of legal mining, but that illegal mining was a far bigger problem.

At Ecuador's first Bio Summit held Nov. 4 to Nov. 6 in Quito — an event aimed at helping the country's natural assets "enter the global financial system" — a new way of monetizing nature was introduced.

Marcelo Salame, the event's president, told CNBC he is working on a project to bring so-called biodiversity credits to the country for the first time, to "preserve and monetize" a 260-hectare, or 640-acre, area of the Prosperina forest owned by ESPOL University in the port city of Guayaquil.

Salame said biodiversity is "multidimensional," with credits reflecting a whole ecosystem rather than carbon emissions alone.

Biodiversity credits work in a similar way to the carbon credits that large companies buy to meet sustainability goals, but track a range of measures such as the number of species in a given area or tree canopy cover, with the money raised funneled back into nature protection.

But whereas carbon credits can be used to offset emissions, biodiversity credits cannot. "It's all about funding verified nature outcomes and not, to date at least, compensating for damage," Martin Vaquié, co-founder of Berlin-based Bloomlabs, a company that tracks the biodiversity market, told CNBC.

Jerry Toth, a conservationist and co-founder of Ecuador-based Third Millennium Alliance, a nonprofit that aims to protect the "last remnants" of Ecuador's Pacific Forest, told CNBC that carbon markets can reduce the ecological value of a forest to a single metric: how many tons of carbon it stores.

"That can be potentially dangerous because then you have people that are optimizing for this one metric at the expense of all the other things," he said. Toth hopes biodiversity credits can capture the nuances of an ecosystem.

Toth also runs chocolate maker To'ak — which made headlines for producing the "world's most expensive chocolate bar" — using ancient Nacional cacao grown in Ecuador's Manabí province and processed in Quito.

Third Millennium Alliance works with farmers to restore degraded land by planting cacao in the shade of taller trees, and To'ak pays them around $1.20 per pound of cacao, about double the current market price, Toth said.

Ensuring that chocolate production does not deplete nature is "the key question," Toth said. "Our goal at the outset was, how do we use chocolate to reverse deforestation?"

Ecuador recognizes its "immense" ecological and biological resources, Toth said. "It's still trying to figure out how to … capture that value in a way that makes sense both for people in Ecuador and for the ecosystem."

#2891. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2887. Nvidia is done with Jensen Huang. The fallout in Washington is just beginning

Jensen Huang is out at Nvidia. Washington is just getting started with him and the company he co-founded.

Huang, the leather-jacket-clad CEO of the world's most important chipmaker, resigned from Nvidia on Thursday after revelations that he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy. But the spy saga is gripping Washington, and lawmakers are preparing for investigations into how the now-ex-CEO's affair may have damaged U.S. artificial intelligence dominance, the national security implications and the executive's close relationship with President Donald Trump and his Cabinet.

Republicans will have their chance first during their last six weeks controlling both chambers of Congress. GOP leaders immediately summoned Huang to Capitol Hill and said they would subpoena him if he doesn't agree to show up. In January, Democrats, who are already eager to make their mark with oversight when they take over the U.S. House and Senate, will be in the driver's seat.

"In this environment where there's some really high stakes when it comes to our national security, I would expect there would be some follow-up, yeah," Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., told reporters at the Capitol on Thursday.

Huang and the woman with whom he reportedly had a relationship, Alice Huang, an executive at the Singaporean technology company Megaspeed and an alleged Chinese spy have drawn congressional attention. And Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has drawn fire after reports this week that he stalled President Donald Trump's crackdowns on firms routing Nvidia chips to China.

Some Congress members have also called for an investigation into the role of the Bureau of Industry and Security, a subagency of the Commerce Department that manages and enforces export controls, in the Huang scandal.

The New York Times reported on Monday that Nvidia's general counsel, Tim Teter, claimed in emails to Commerce investigators probing Alice Huang's company that he'd been in touch with Jeffrey Kessler, who leads BIS. The Commerce investigation into Megaspeed, which allegedly smuggled restricted chips to China, was then transferred to the Justice Department.

By Thursday night, Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., summoned both Huang and Lutnick to testify before his committee in December. Later that same night, Lutnick had "proactively agreed to appear voluntarily before the Banking Committee to address their questions," Kristen Eichamer, a Commerce Department spokesperson, said in a statement.

Scott threatened to subpoena Huang if he does not appear. A banking panel spokesperson did not immediately respond Friday morning when asked if they'd heard from Huang.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive U.S. technology," Scott wrote to Huang. "The American people deserve answers."

And with Democrats poised to take control of both chambers come January, the tech mogul's close ties to the White House will add fuel to an already burning fire as the new majority spools up myriad investigations into the Trump administration.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., in a post to X on Thursday, said "Congress must demand answers from Mr. Huang and others at Nvidia about how they may have put U.S. national security at risk." Schumer is in a contested race to become Senate majority leader in the next Congress.

The senators who will lead those investigations, and hold subpoena power in the new Congress, are gearing up for Nvidia to take center stage.

Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the likely incoming chair of the Senate banking panel, said in a statement Thursday that "Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and NVIDIA's board of directors still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls

Warren and Sen. Chris Coons, D-Del., the top Democrat on the Senate Foreign Relations subpanel with jurisdiction over East Asia and the Pacific, sent a letter Wednesday to Lutnick, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller instructing them to preserve all documents and communications related to Nvidia's exports of "advanced AI chips to China or to entities that may divert such chips to China."

Even before Democrats take control in January, Huang is expected to appear before Congress.

House Foreign Affairs Committee Chair Brian Mast, R-Fla., is actively negotiating for Huang to testify before House committees and told reporters Thursday the Nvidia CEO has agreed to appear in the coming weeks.

Details of that testimony are still being worked out, Mast said. It would likely include his committee, as well as the House intelligence panel and the House Permanent Select Committee on China, and it would be under oath and transcribed. It's not clear at this point whether it would be public.

"We want to see him basically in a week … Sooner rather than later," Mast told reporters Thursday, adding that he believed it would happen before Thanksgiving.

If Huang doesn't willingly appear, Mast said he will subpoena him.

Mast, along with House Select China Committee Chair Rep. John Moolenaar, R-Mich., and House Intelligence Committee Chair Rep. Rick Crawford, R-Ark., sent a second request Thursday to Huang and Teter requesting they appear and giving them an 8 p.m. ET Friday deadline to respond. Mast also sent a second request to Kessler.

Rep. Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., a member of the House Intelligence Committee, said Friday during an appearance on CNBC's "The Exchange" that he wants answers from Huang on the national security and export implications of the affair.

"From an intelligence committee perspective, you can imagine from an AI perspective, how important this is for our national security, for our critical infrastructure and understand when it comes to chip policy, what exactly got to China and ... did Megaspeed divert chips?" Gottheimer said.

The calls for immediate investigation into Huang and Nvidia on the day of his resignation represent a stunning fall from grace in the halls of power for the former CEO. Huang was one of the most effective voices for the AI industry in Washington — able to toggle between leveraging his relationship with Trump and defending his industry from fears about AI's risks to lawmakers.

"He is always the TED Talk guy," one senior Democratic staff member who was granted anonymity to describe private meetings they had attended with Huang, told CNBC. "He's really good and articulate, and really good at explaining why AI is important and why it's so important for the United States to be leading here."

The staffer said Huang was a "happy warrior" for AI, and stayed longer than scheduled or anticipated for questions from lawmakers. But in the meetings with lawmakers the aide attended, they said Huang did not aggressively push against export controls or get too "deep in the weeds" to really "lobby one way or the other" with the lawmakers.

Huang has repeatedly resisted export controls publicly, a move that has rubbed some lawmakers the wrong way.

Some lawmakers even lamented the loss of Huang, despite the inevitable buzzsaw he now faces on Capitol Hill.

"It's just sad all around," Sen. John Fetterman, D-Pa., said of Huang's resignation.

#2883. Analysis: Companies love a ‘rockstar’ CEO. Until they depend on one

When Jensen Huang quit, it struck me how quickly the conversation shifted from his departure to whether Nvidia could manage without him.

It wasn't about whether the company had the technology, customers or talent to keep growing, but whether anyone else could do what Huang had been doing for the business. That made me wonder: did investors believe in Nvidia, or just Huang?

The company is hardly short of capable people. Interim CEO and Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress has been with Nvidia since 2013. Nvidia has experienced engineers, established customer relationships and a leading position in the artificial intelligence chip market.

But, listening to guests on CNBC this week, I noticed how much of their concern was about the influence Huang had built outside Nvidia.

On Friday, just a day after Huang quit, Dylan Patel of SemiAnalysis told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" how Huang would persuade executives at smaller companies that make critical components to commit capacity to Nvidia. This helped it secure "the majority of supply chain capacity," he said.

Without Huang, competitors could try to persuade those suppliers to allocate more capacity to them, Patel argued. And so the big question is, how much of Nvidia's advantage comes from its technology and how much comes from the relationships Huang built?

There are obvious benefits to elevating CEOs to be rockstars. They can attract investors, open doors and give a company a new identity. A charismatic leader attracts attention, reassures investors and makes a company's strategy easier to sell.

Huang was particularly good at this. He helped shape the conversation about where AI was heading and made Nvidia central to it. Donning his trademark leather jacket, Huang took to stages around the world, courting world leaders to lay out his vision and explain why Nvidia would be at its center.

Before Huang resigned, Eric Ross told "Squawk Box Asia" that Huang "is the company."

That may sound like an endorsement, but it also helps explain why his departure has raised so many questions.

Bernstein's Stacy Rasgon made an interesting distinction on the same program, also before Huang's departure. Nvidia had capable executives who could keep the business running, he said. What was harder to replace was Huang's vision.

"They need a visionary like that," he said.

But for a company whose valuation reflects not just what it sells but expectations of what AI could become, replacing that vision may prove harder than replacing the CEO.

Duncan Clark pointed to another part of Huang's job. He had become a bridge between Silicon Valley, Asia and Washington, and one of the most prominent voices for the wider AI industry.

"I don't think you can replace him with one individual," Clark said on "Access Middle East" on Friday. Replacing him with multiple people is "going to take time, and it won't be the same," he added.

That distinction may be important. Nvidia's board has begun a search for a permanent CEO and said it will consider both internal and external candidates. But whoever takes the job may not personally assume every role Huang had come to play around the company. Some of his responsibilities and relationships could end up being spread across Nvidia's broader leadership team.

For now, the board is emphasizing continuity. New chair Harvey Jones said Kress is backed by an experienced leadership team and that Nvidia expected its operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption. Kress, meanwhile, pointed to Nvidia's people and leadership team as strengths as the company moves forward.

Other boards will be watching, particularly those that have allowed their CEOs to become the face of everything their companies do.

When a charismatic leader is the only person outsiders hear from, it becomes harder to convince them that anyone else can take over. There is also the question of accountability.

Clark described Nvidia's situation as a governance failure. And my colleague, "Squawk Box Europe" anchor Steve Sedgwick, raised a similar point on Friday about how corporate America has approached ESG.

Much of the political debate has focused on its environmental component, Steve said. President Donald Trump has repeatedly attacked environmental and climate policies using phrases such as "green scam." But Steve questioned whether the governance element has been overlooked, particularly at some of America's biggest technology companies.

"When you have a 'Rockstar' CEO you need way more governance, NOT less," he said, citing his recent conversations with CEOs and senior executives.

His point was that powerful leaders can come to believe the normal rules don't apply to them. Without strong governance to challenge them and prepare for their departure, the consequences for a company can be far greater.

Linda Ford, chief executive of The Chartered Governance Institute in the U.K., said Nvidia showed what could happen "when a company's leadership is built around one person." She noted that Nvidia had never previously had a board chair, adding: "A high-profile leader can be an asset, but the board needs to work hard to protect its independence and provide the scrutiny shareholders are entitled to expect."

And that gets to the bigger issue. How easily can a board challenge a CEO who has delivered extraordinary success, particularly when investors have come to see that person as indispensable?

Companies spend years putting leaders in front of investors and customers and making them central to the business. But how much effort goes into making sure the company can function without them?

Nvidia now has to show that the business Huang built can stand on its own. Other boards might want to ask themselves whether they could say the same.

#2879. Microsoft apologizes for massive outage that took down Outlook and other 365 services

Microsoft issued an apology Friday for a wide-ranging outage that shut down Outlook and other Microsoft 365 products, as the company worked to restore service nearly 24 hours after connectivity problems began.

"We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners. We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously," a Microsoft spokesperson said Friday.

On Saturday, Microsoft said it had addressed the underlying issue and restored service to the remaining impacted customers and regions.

The connectivity disruption stemmed from "an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade," Microsoft said in a Friday statement. The software giant added that the underlying cause of the outage had been addressed and that they were still working to restore services to some customers.

Engineers scrambled to solve connectivity problems that began shortly before 1 p.m. ET on Thursday.

Email and calendar service Outlook, along with multiple Microsoft 365 services such as Sharepoint and Copilot, were largely down during the morning hours in the U.S. on Friday and throughout the Asian and European business days. Numerous external services that connect with Microsoft products were also hit. Users began to regain access to email and other services Friday morning.

In an update around 10:30 a.m. ET on Friday, Microsoft noted "encouraging recovery progress" and said that "service availability and email delivery performance [was] improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure" as Outlook and other Microsoft 365 tools began to come back online.

In an earlier update Friday morning, Microsoft said that email queues were "approaching normal levels," but that intermittent issues were expected as recovery efforts continue. It had previously given updates by region, with Asia Pacific services among the first to start normalizing.

Engineering teams have been taking affected systems offline for fixes and bringing them back online gradually stages. They are prioritizing efforts to preserve emails delayed during the recovery process and reduce email queues and backlogs, extending the timeout window, whereby delayed emails continue to attempt delivery, to 48 hours to help protect messages.

Microsoft says it has not detected any malicious activity causing the outage, which it initially blamed on a traffic surge in part of its infrastructure and issues with a component responsible for processing server health. The company said it would issue a full report once its assessment of the outage was complete.

The company said in its initial updates on Thursday that the connectivity problems related to infrastructure "elevated load across multiple regions," caused by a "number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable; straining the remaining infrastructure." Some servers were failing automated health checks, it said.

Microsoft shares traded largely flat Friday.

Governments and businesses across the U.S., Asia-Pacific and Europe said they were monitoring the situation, which was causing widespread disruption.

The U.K.'s National Health Service said the outage was affecting some of its services but that patients should attend appointments as normal. Calls "may be subject to long waits" as a result of disruption to the organization, a hospital group serving South West London and the English county of Surrey said on X.

Earnings for Swiss luxury goods giant Richemont were slightly delayed on Friday due to the outage. Electric truck maker Workhorse Group, which was expected to report third-quarter results on Nov. 12, rescheduled the release to Nov. 16, citing business disruptions caused by the global Microsoft outage.

U.S. Rep. Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., told CNBC's "The Exchange" that he asked for a briefing on the outage. Gottheimer worked at Microsoft prior to serving in Congress.

"Is there something that went on that's nefarious here, or is this just something else? These outages occur in a lot of the companies, but the size of it and the length of it and the timing of it," Gottheimer said.

Air India said it had "temporarily rerouted" emails so it could continue to receive customer messages, adding that its flight operations were not affected.

Australian Home Affairs and Cyber Security Minister Tony Burke said in a press conference that Microsoft "believe they are dealing in some ways similar to what we saw with respect to CrowdStrike where there is an update that has gone awry," Reuters reported.

A fault with an update issued by cybersecurity company CrowdStrike caused one of the world's biggest IT system outages in 2024, hitting airlines, banks and more.

Microsoft reported in 2025 that Copilot, its collection of AI assistants and tools, had more than 100 million monthly active users.

The incident highlights a business "resilience and dependency issue that has been decades in the making," James Owen, managing partner of Digital Risks at consultancy Control Risks, said by phone.

"We have become, organizationally in the private sector and more broadly, increasingly dependent on a smaller segment of technology and infrastructure as critical to daily life and business activities."

"Organizations are becoming more aware through a geopolitical lens about what technologies they adopt, whether their services are provided by firms in the U.S., or China, or elsewhere, but this shows the importance of proper recovery planning in the case of an IT outage. That could be manual backups, protocols that do not require internet connection, investing in diverse suppliers and crisis management planning rehearsals," he said.

Amazon Web Services and Google users also reported outages on the site on Thursday.

Amazon wrote on X that services were "operating normally" and an "unrelated disruption elsewhere on the internet is prompting some inaccurate news reports." The company referred users to its health dashboard for "accurate data."

At 4 p.m. ET Thursday, Microsoft said it had "validated an alternative service health check method" that brought some affected servers back online.

"We're now applying this change more broadly," Microsoft wrote. "We're also preparing automated recovery systems to continually detect and restart unresponsive servers, to maintain service stability while this change progresses."

#2877. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2873. After Jensen Huang’s exit, what top financial advisors are doing with Nvidia: Exclusive CNBC poll

Shares of Nvidia continued to slide after the market open on Friday after declining 4% on Thursday following news of co-founder Jensen Huang's resignation as chief executive, amid reports about his personal relationships and national security concerns. The news has raised questions for investors about what comes next for a company at the center of the artificial intelligence boom.

To gauge how wealth managers are responding, CNBC polled members of its Financial Advisor 100 and Financial Advisor Council. Thirty of the 122 firms and professionals contacted responded to the online, multiple-choice flash poll, conducted between 11 a.m. and 5 p.m. EST on Nov. 12. The poll is a snapshot of respondents' views, not a measure of all financial advisors.

Most respondents, 19, said they are holding their Nvidia positions steady. Another four said they are increasing their stake, while seven said they do not own the stock. None said they were selling at the time of the poll.

"Most of our clients are in a position to hold; the reason is because it makes up a smaller percentage of an overall diversified portfolio," Winnie Sun, managing director and founding partner of Irvine, California-based Sun Group Wealth Partners, said in an interview. Sun is a member of the CNBC Financial Advisor Council.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

In open responses, several advisors wrote that they are taking a "wait-and-see" approach before changing their investment strategy with Nvidia and are telling their clients to do the same.

"Don't react to short-term headlines," wrote certified financial planner Peyton Jamison, president of Jamison Private Wealth Management in Alpharetta, Georgia. "The company's valuation is extremely attractive, and the focus should remain on its underlying fundamentals and long-term investment potential." His firm is ranked No. 78 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

Asked what factors could change their approach to Nvidia, 13 respondents cited the outlook for AI demand, 11 pointed to valuation, and 10 mentioned company leadership or governance. Some said the decision depends on the client, with eight citing risk tolerance and seven mentioning portfolio concentration. Seven said they do not expect to change their approach.

Nvidia is scheduled to report its third-quarter fiscal 2027 financial results Tuesday, after the market close. In poll remarks, advisors said the company's fiscal third-quarter earnings will help determine their course of action.

Advisors said that for investors, what to do may depend on how much Nvidia stock they already own, when they expect to need the money and how comfortable they are with volatility.

"If this is a company that's going to keep you up at night and you just feel really uncomfortable with these unknown risks, then there was always the option to sell and move that capital to another stock or another portfolio," Sun said.

Nvidia exposure can also come through mutual funds and ETFs. Nvidia's large market value makes it a significant holding in many broad-market funds, including funds that track the S&P 500, as well as target-date funds in retirement-plan options.

Investors can check their Nvidia exposure by reviewing the fact sheets for the funds in their 401(k) or brokerage accounts, which typically list a fund's largest holdings. A stock's decline does not translate one-for-one into a fund's decline.

However, investors with dedicated technology, semiconductor or growth funds may have more Nvidia exposure than they realize, advisors say.

"Nvidia is a big part of virtually everyone's portfolio whether they take an active or passive index approach," wrote Andy Pratt, a partner and director of investment strategy at Burney Wealth Management in Reston, Virginia, which is ranked No. 34 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

He said he advises clients not to overreact to short-term news and to let the dust settle before making decisions.

— CNBC's Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting to this article.

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2867. Oliver Kahn fires back at Bayern over Musiala: "Loyalty is not a contractual obligation!"

Oliver Kahn has taken aim at Bayern Munich's handling of Jamal Musiala's health situation, delivering a strong message about loyalty, responsibility and player welfare. Following Uli Hoeneß's recent doubts about whether the 23-year-old can ever return to his previous level, the former Bayern CEO has offered a different perspective.

"Contractual loyalty is not loyalty. It is an obligation," Kahn wrote in a LinkedIn post. He argued that a club's true commitment only becomes apparent when a player can no longer deliver on the pitch.

"Clubs like to call themselves families. The problem starts when they talk about family as long as it costs nothing, and switch to talking numbers as soon as it becomes expensive," the 57-year-old added.

Kahn went even further, insisting that fulfilling a contract should not be celebrated as an extraordinary gesture of goodwill. "A club should not pretend that honouring the contract it signed itself is an act of particular humanity. The club did not just buy the performance. It also bought the risk."

The former goalkeeper also drew parallels with Manuel Neuer's serious leg injury in December 2022. Bayern publicly stood behind their captain before signing Yann Sommer just six weeks later.

"Both messages reach the player. But the second one is always heard much louder," Kahn concluded.

Musiala remains under contract until 2030, but has endured a difficult period with injuries and neurological issues. Bayern have stressed that continuing his playing career remains medically justifiable.

#2866. Rising rents draw Wall Street money to industrial outdoor storage lot investments across the U.S.

Nondescript lots holding idle bulldozers, trucks and other industrial equipment are a common sight in local real estate markets across the U.S., and increasingly, a focus for Wall Street private investment giants.

If you haven't noticed them, it's probably because they so easily fade into the landscape, just another non-branded bit of land in the miasma of suburbia. But Brookfield and Blackstone, two of the world's largest alternative asset managers, are among the institutional investors moving in on what the industry calls industrial outdoor storage, or IOS. Investors are eyeing these lots as the AI build-out increases the demand for heavy equipment.

"We have strong conviction in industrial outdoor storage, which plays a critical role in our supply chain," said Devin Barnwell, a managing partner and co-leader of Brookfield's logistics business.

He says that up until now, the segment has been fragmented, but Brookfield sees that changing.

"We see significant opportunity to bring institutional capital and operating expertise to this fragmented sector, improving the quality and efficiency of these assets while strengthening the resilience of our industrial supply chains," Barnwell said.

Brookfield spent $1.2 billion earlier this year to take Peakstone Realty Trust private, a deal completed in May that gave it a portfolio of more than 70 industrial properties, most of them outdoor storage yards.

Blackstone is also ramping up in the sector, mainly as a lender. Its real estate debt arm has made six loans to outdoor storage owners totaling more than $1.1 billion, including a $244 million loan in June to Alterra IOS to buy 37 properties.

Despite the billions being bet on the market by Wall Street, there is still opportunity in the market for individual investors and local entrepreneurs looking to park their money where others park their equipment.

"1,000 percent yes," said Adam Petrillo, an executive managing director at Newmark, a commercial real estate advisory firm, who co-leads its industrial outdoor storage practice.

Petrillo said smaller, non-institutional investors, still control a significant amount of well-placed storage in secondary markets. "The good news for smaller investors and mom and pops is that a good individual IOS site isn't necessarily 50 acres," Petrillo said.

Lots as small as three to five acres in primary or secondary markets can still yield a robust return, he said.  "At that bite size, an individual can step in. We are not talking about tens of millions ... a three-acre, well-located IOS with multiple use cases can be found, marketed, leased,  and stabilized," he added.

Rents for industrial outdoor storage roughly doubled across eight major markets between 2020 and the third quarter of this year, according to data from Newmark Research and Alterra IOS, an owner of such properties. Average rent per usable acre rose 103.5%, to nearly $11,000 a month, compared with a 66% increase for traditional industrial space, such as indoor warehouses.

Rent growth, according to Alterra, is most closely tied to infrastructure and energy activity in the area, even more so than data center development specifically.

The value of these properties can also increase if located near a seaport, rail line, or highway. But the gains weren't uniform. Baltimore (204%), Houston (159%) and Norfolk (148%) led, while South Florida and Savannah IOS actually trailed traditional industrial.

Lisa DeNight, managing director, head of North American industrial research at Newmark, said that Savannah's IOS vacancy remains quite tight despite the rent figures. Recent port activity is a potential factor, according to Alterra. Savannah's container volumes more than doubled between 2010 and 2022 but have since declined, while Houston saw roughly 15% growth from 2022 to 2025.

Petrillo said that there is still plenty of room left for values to increase, so an investor jumping in now hasn't missed the game. "We are still in the very early innings," Petrillo said.

Sam Cheema co-founder of CanTex Capital, a Dallas-based firm that acquires overlooked industrial real estate in Texas, is seeing increased demand for these lots.

"It comes down to supply and demand," he said. "If you need a warehouse you can usually find one. Properly zoned infill IOS sites are much harder to find and every year more of them are converted to warehouses and other uses," he said, referencing multifamily housing units. The scarcity makes tenants sticky. "So a limited supply keeps shrinking. And because so much of the value is in the land itself, owners keep the option to redevelop the site as the area grows," Cheema added.

Because of the imbalance of supply and demand, rents and prices are rising across the board, according to Cheema, but no two IOS properties look alike, which has kept the door open for smaller investors.

"Big institutions want exposure to IOS, but ownership is fragmented across mom-and-pop owners and small businesses, so it's hard to deploy large amounts of capital without first aggregating these assets into portfolios," Cheema said.

Unlike traditional warehouses, there's no standard spec like square footage or clear height to compare them. "That lack of standardization along with the ongoing growth of the asset class, means there's still runway for small investors to find opportunities," Cheema said.

"Financing has improved as more lenders have a better understanding of IOS, but it's still largely local and regional banks," Cheema added.

"If you don't understand the city's plans for the area, you may end up owning a use they're trying to phase out," Cheema said.

CanTex Capital works with the city and neighborhoods before buying to ensure the impact aligns with the community's plan for its future.

Alex Sutterer, an executive vice president at NAI Hiffman, a Chicago-area commercial real estate firm, said that beyond being a place to store big equipment, IOS attracts investors for two main reasons: lower capital expenditure requirements than traditional industrial or multifamily properties and the potential for higher yields.

"Capital improvements are also less specific to individual tenants, so owners can direct spending toward the property's overall functionality," Sutterer said.

For private investors, IOS typically trades at a yield around one percentage point higher than traditional industrial properties, helping attract smaller investors and family offices. "We believe that gap will continue to narrow as more institutional capital enters this space," Sutterer added.

Even for industrial properties with similar yields, he said the credit profile of tenants is generally stronger for IOS.

Shallow-bay, multitenant industrial properties — industrial facilities that tend to be much smaller than big-box distribution warehouses — have comparable yields. But unlike the local businesses that tend to fill up shallow-bay sites, IOS tenants range from local businesses to those with investment-grade credit ratings, offering investors the potential for both yield and creditworthy tenants.

And institutional money is making IOS more competitive.

"Institutional capital has increased competition in IOS, and property values and lease rates have risen overall," Sutterer said.

He added smaller investors can still find opportunities below institutional deal-size thresholds, although that does not mean every smaller deal is priced fairly. "We see opportunities to add value and earn yield in deals below $3 million–$5 million," Sutterer said.

For local investors, that means there is the ability to build a portfolio at a smaller scale and hold the assets for cash flow, or aggregate more than $10 million in assets and seek a portfolio premium when selling to institutional capital.

But buyers need to understand what they are getting into. Due diligence on tenant demand by site and site function, and tenant turnover ratio, are important factors in buying the right asset. Market diversity can also provide protection, Sutterer said.

"In Chicago, no single user category accounts for more than 18% of IOS tenants, helping insulate the market from industry-specific slowdowns," he said. Tenants' tendency to stay and tight vacancy rates nationally are current advantages of the asset class. In Chicagoland, for example, vacancy is below 3%.

Still, all the due diligence in the world can't protect investors from some variables.

"No one outside the Federal Reserve can control the cost of debt," said Kellie McIvor, vice president of acquisitions at Redwood Living, an Ohio real estate development firm. She said that right now, the cost of debt for light industrial uses is lower than, say, for residential construction.

"However, interest rates are still high, with no relief in sight," McIvor said.

The Fed is expected to raise rates in December in its effort to tame inflation, and after this week's latest hot October CPI report, potentially hike twice more in early 2027, according to some Wall Street forecasts.

"Because lenders view them as riskier than a large institutional investor, smaller buyers can face more expensive debt and may be asked to bring more equity to the deal. That can make financing a small acquisition disproportionately expensive. ... More expensive debt means less room for error in execution," McIvor said.

A small buyer looking at a small site that requires no improvement may be able to fund the deal all on his own. "That's the dream! Own your business and the land it's on, outright!" she added.

Ross Blankenship, who owns two fenced contractor and fleet yards in Colorado's Vail Valley through Wins Parking, said the math has to work today. In a mountain market where little land is zoned for the use, he rents spaces for $210 a month for a car or pickup and $265 for a box truck or trailer, and charges $1,250 to $5,500 a month for contractor zones. Blankenship also runs Park Graph, the parking software he uses to rent the spaces.

The swing factor is vacancy, he said. "A full yard throws off more cash per dollar of capex than a small retail or office building. An empty one earns nothing," he said.

His advice for a first-time buyer: call 10 local contractors and ask what they pay for space.

"A small investor can still buy, but the deal has to work on rents you can collect today, not on what an institution might pay you later," Blankenship said.
