#2808. Social Security and the blue wave: What could happen to payroll taxes and senior tax breaks

Congress has six years to enact changes to Social Security to prevent cuts to retirement benefits. Senators elected in the Nov. 3 midterms will be in office when the deadline arrives, and their decisions may be critical in making tax changes that can affect Social Security.

During the midterm campaign season, tax policy surfaced in the debate over Social Security in two different ways. Many Democrats, and some Republicans, called for high earners to pay more in Social Security payroll taxes, while many Republicans touted a recently enacted tax deduction for adults 65 and older.

The diverging tactics come at a crucial moment for reform: The first could increase revenue flowing into the program. The second reduces eligible older adults' income taxes and can lower the taxes some beneficiaries pay on Social Security benefits, reducing program revenue.

Social Security trustees' latest projections show the trust fund used to pay retirement benefits may start to become depleted in 2032, when 78% of benefits would be payable.

Lawmakers do have some leeway — if the program's retirement and disability trust funds are combined, depletion would be pushed out to 2034, when 83% of scheduled benefits would be payable. But that's still a haircut for retirees — and it's coming soon.

"We've waited too long already, and the consequences are that the gap has grown larger," Shai Akabas, vice president of economic policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, said of Social Security's shortfall.

On the campaign trail, midterm candidates largely avoided specifics on how they would reform Social Security. However, raising the payroll tax cap, also known as the taxable maximum, was a popular choice among Democratic candidates. That would require people who earn more than the cap, currently $184,500, to begin contributing payroll taxes for their earnings above that amount.

Meanwhile, some Republican candidates said they would extend the temporary senior tax deduction of up to $6,000 per eligible individual that is set to expire in 2028.

The deduction, part of the One Big Beautiful Bill Act passed in July of last year and billed as a senior bonus, was among the factors that moved Social Security trustees' retirement trust fund depletion projections three months sooner, according to an annual report released in June.

Here's what experts say could be in store now that Democrats have won control of the House and Senate.

Social Security is a pay-as-you-go system that relies on payroll taxes from workers to help make monthly benefit payments to millions of Americans.

In 2026, earnings up to $184,500 are subject to that tax. Workers and employers each contribute 6.2% of pay up to that threshold, while self-employed individuals pay 12.4%.

The wage cap will rise in 2027 to $192,300. It adjusts higher annually to reflect incremental changes in the average wage index.

On the campaign trail, some House and Senate candidates said the cap should be raised or eliminated entirely so high earners contribute more.

Following the election, Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., who is expected to become the next chair of the Social Security subcommittee, said on X on Nov. 7 that his proposal to increase benefits by at least $2,400 per year and "scrap the cap" on earnings subject to Social Security taxes would be a "top priority."

Outgoing Sen. Bill Cassidy, R-La., responded on X that the plan would "put a 12.4% tax" on people who are not millionaires or billionaires.

The idea of higher payroll taxes has broader support. A national survey fielded in February found most respondents — 79% — support raising taxes for wages over $400,000, according to the University of Maryland School of Public Policy. The change was the most popular option tested among the 2,509 adults surveyed.

Rising income inequality has meant the payroll tax cap no longer covers 90% of earnings, though that percentage was expected to remain constant when lawmakers enacted the last major reforms to the program in 1983, according to research from the Roosevelt Institute, a New York-based liberal think tank. The share of earnings covered by Social Security payroll taxes fell to around 83% in 2000 and has mostly stabilized at that level, according to the institute.

Despite candidates mentioning plans to change the cap during election season, some experts say Congress may put off tax hikes that could be politically unpopular.

"I think lawmakers may put some of these taxes on the table closer to 2032, when the Social Security trust fund hits insolvency," said Jessica Riedl, a fellow at the Urban-Brookings Tax Policy Center, which provides independent analyses of tax issues. "In Washington, they love to wait until the last minute."

If the payroll tax cap were lifted from just under $200,000 to $300,000 in earnings per year, that would cost someone at that higher income threshold about $6,700 more in direct payroll taxes, Riedl said.

"There's no interest on either side for these sorts of tax increases in the short term," Riedl said. In the long term, though, "everything is on the table," she said.

While many Democrats have pushed for raising those taxes, some Republicans have also shared their openness to doing so.

"There's a crack in the dam there breaking," Dan Adcock, director of government relations and policy at the National Committee to Preserve Social Security and Medicare, said of the Republican support.

Even if lawmakers raise or eliminate the cap, they would still need to agree on other changes to shore up the program. Republicans may want benefit cuts such as raising the retirement age in exchange for increasing taxes, Adcock said.

Just how far adjusting the taxable maximum would go to help solve Social Security's funding woes depends on how it is applied.

If the cap is eliminated starting in 2027, with the full 12.4% tax applying to all earnings and no benefit credit for earnings above the current threshold, that would eliminate 58% of the long-range actuarial balance over 75 years, according to a September analysis by the Social Security Administration.

Applying the 12.4% payroll tax rate to earnings over $250,000 starting in 2027 could address 57% of the long-range balance, according to the SSA, provided there are no benefit increases in exchange for the extra contributions.

If the 12.4% payroll tax rate were applied to earnings over $400,000 starting in 2027 without benefit increases, that would eliminate 53% of the long-range actuarial balance, the agency's analysis found.

Eventually, all earnings would be subject to Social Security payroll taxes as the gap between the taxable maximum, which increases every year, and the new threshold closes over time.

Yet some experts say raising the payroll tax cap could have consequences for taxpayers and the economy.

Raising the cap would be an "unprecedented increase" in marginal tax rates, according to William McBride, chief economist at the Tax Foundation, an independent tax policy research organization.

Lifting the threshold could add more than 12 percentage points to the cost of earning an additional dollar of wages, McBride said. Those higher costs could affect the labor market, which may see higher unemployment as a result of the change, he said.

OBBBA's new senior tax deduction of up to $6,000 per individual — or "bonus additional amount for seniors," as it was dubbed in an early version of the legislation — is in effect for tax years 2025 through 2028.

Lawmakers elected to Congress in the midterms may decide whether to extend the temporary tax break.

"2028 is going to be a presidential election year, and I would not expect either party to be willing to allow any senior tax cuts to expire while they're competing for senior votes," Riedl said.

When President Donald Trump signed OBBBA into law in 2025, the White House said in a release: "No tax on Social Security is a reality." However, the new law does not eliminate taxes on Social Security benefits.

Instead, it provides a deduction that reduces seniors' taxable income regardless of whether they have claimed Social Security benefits.

During the campaign, Republicans touted the $6,000 senior deduction as tax relief and criticized Democrats for voting against the law that enacted it. However, congressional Democrats unanimously opposed OBBBA, citing provisions including cuts to Medicaid and food assistance.

Some effects of the new $6,000 senior deduction are different from those of eliminating taxes on benefits, according to McBride at the Tax Foundation. The senior deduction affects lower- and middle-income taxpayers to a greater degree, while eliminating taxes on Social Security benefits would be more beneficial to upper-income taxpayers, McBride said.

How Social Security benefits are currently taxed

Individuals with $25,000 to $34,000 in combined income — the sum of adjusted gross income, tax-exempt interest and half of annual Social Security benefits — pay federal taxes on up to 50% of their Social Security benefits. For individuals with over $34,000 in combined income, up to 85% of their Social Security benefits are subject to federal income tax.

For married couples filing jointly with between $32,000 and $44,000 in combined income, up to 50% of their Social Security benefits are subject to federal income tax. For married couples with more than $44,000 in combined income, that increases to up to 85% of their benefits.

The senior bonus tax deduction allows those age 65 and older to deduct up to $6,000 for individuals, or up to $12,000 for married couples. The deduction phases out for modified adjusted gross income over $75,000 for single filers and $150,000 for married couples.

The senior bonus deduction — together with the tax year 2025 standard deduction of $15,750 for single taxpayers and $31,500 for married couples and the existing senior deduction for those who qualify of $2,000 for singles and $3,200 for married couples — "amounts to the largest tax break in history for America's seniors," the White House said in July 2025. In 2026, the standard deduction is $16,100 for single filers and $32,200 for married couples.

Under OBBBA, 88% of beneficiaries, or 51.4 million individuals age 65 and older receiving Social Security income, will have exemptions and deductions that exceed their taxable Social Security income, according to the administration. Prior to the law, 64% of beneficiaries, or 37.2 million individuals, had tax breaks that exceeded their taxable Social Security income.

But the measure does nothing to resolve Social Security's depletion problem.

In an annual report released in June, Social Security's trustees cited OBBBA as having a "substantial effect" on the program's financial status because beneficiaries will pay less in income taxes on benefits. In that report, the trustees moved the projected depletion date for the program's retirement trust fund to the fourth quarter of 2032 — up one quarter from the previous year's projections.

"This new deduction for seniors cuts into some of the funding for the trust fund, so it makes the solvency date a little nearer in time," McBride said. "It makes the program a little more fragile, if you will."

#2807. Kyrie Irving was recognized in Cleveland, in honor of his Game 7 shot to secure the 2016 NBA Finals

Kyrie Irving got a moment in the spotlight during the first timeout of Saturday's game between the Dallas Mavericks (7-5) and the Cleveland Cavaliers (6-7) at Rocket Arena.

The home crowd roared in appreciation as Irving's step-back 3-pointer to seal the Cavaliers' Game 7 win over the Golden State Warriors in the 2016 NBA Finals was replayed on the video boards at midcourt.

Irving tapped his chest and thanked the crowd as the Mavs left their huddle to return to the floor with 7:32 left in the first quarter. He may not have left the Cavaliers on the best terms a season later, but that shot proved to be the clincher in the franchise's first and only NBA Championship. He asked for a trade in the 2017 offseason, and the Cavaliers sent him to the Boston Celtics in exchange for Isaiah Thomas, Jae Crowder, Ante Žižić, and a 2018 first-round draft pick.

The Cavs won Game 7, 93-89, scoring just 18 points in the fourth quarter that night. They held Stephen Curry and the Warriors to just 13 points down the stretch. Irving scored five, and someone named LeBron James had 11 as Cleveland claimed the title behind their own version of Batman and Robin.

"I wanted to prove everybody wrong, and I wanted to prove myself right," Irving said in an interview with NBA.com before the game. "What a time. Shoutout to all of my teammates that were part of that team. It was a hard-fought season. I was coming off injury, going through a lot of emotions throughout the year and just being uncertain whether I could get back to the level I believed I could get back to. After we won, I was still like, 'Is there more?' Now when I look at this [shot], there's just this appreciation of my youth, my fearlessness — things I attribute my success to today. Game 7, NBA Finals — that's the pinnacle of any player's career. "

He rose up over Curry, kept his right elbow tucked and sank one of the most historic shots in NBA Finals history as a 24-year-old that night, just five years into what has blossomed into a 15-year, Hall of Fame-worthy career. He scored 26 points and pulled down six rebounds that night, just two games after putting together a 41-point masterpiece in the Cavs' win in Game 5.

James, who scored a matching 41 points in Game 5 that year, would win NBA Finals MVP as he and Irving brought a title to his hometown team.

The Cleveland crowd also gave Irving an ovation when his name was called during the pregame introductions for the starting lineups.

#2806. SpaceX catches upper stage of Starship rocket in historic first

After a booster explosion on Friday, SpaceX successfully caught the upper stage, or spacecraft, portion of its massive Starship rocket from orbit on Saturday — a triumphant milestone for the company.

SpaceX made the historic catch using its "chopsticks," mechanical arms attached to a launch tower, at its facility in Starbase, Texas.

Test Flight 15, the most ambitious Starship test yet, marked the first time Elon Musk's company attempted to catch both the rocket's booster and upper stage.

Musk posted a video on X inspecting the upper stage craft after completing the capture.

During the first phase of the test flight on Friday, SpaceX failed to catch and recover the booster, instead diverting it to an offshore splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico where it exploded before hitting the water.

The rocket's upper stage then continued into orbit and flew around the Earth 14 times before successfully landing on SpaceX's launch tower, nicknamed Mechazilla.

The Hawaii Department of Transportation issued a temporary pause on some West Coast flights Friday, due to debris risk from the flight. Separately, the FAA issued a rare, nationwide ground stop due to telecommunications system failures. (Both of those orders have since been lifted.)

Making Starship a reliable and fully reusable rocket is critical to SpaceX's ambitions. In the near term, the company needs Starship to send more satellites into orbit to expand its Starlink satellite internet service, which is currently the company's profit center.

SpaceX is also expected to use Starship to bring astronauts and cargo from lunar orbit to the Moon's surface and back for NASA. Musk also has a grand vision to use Starship to "colonize Mars" and "make humanity multi-planetary."

During Test Flight 15 on Friday, SpaceX released another 26 of its newest Starlink v3 satellites into orbit, growing its commercial satellite internet network.

The company's constellation already included over 11,000 satellites in low Earth orbit. One Starship rocket can carry up to 60 Starlink v3 satellites, the company has said.

#2804. Trump breaks silence about FAA nationwide ground stop, blames Biden administration

President Donald Trump on Sunday made his first public comments about Friday's nationwide ground stop by the Federal Aviation Administration, blaming the Biden administration for making an "old and broken" air traffic control system worse. Trump also urged Congress to approve more funding to modernize the system.

The ground stop lasted more than four hours Friday, disrupted flights nationwide, contributed to thousands of cancellations and delays, and affected hundreds of thousands of passengers.

"The Biden Administration was an FAA disaster," Trump wrote on his social media platform. "Big money spent unwisely, making an old and broken system worse than when they began."

The president added that his administration has "the knowledge and plan for the Flight Control Systems," but said that "we need Congress to give the money, FAST!"

Congress approved $12.5 billion for the FAA last year as part of a major tax and spending package. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy has said that the aging air traffic control system will need more funding for a broader overhaul.

The FAA said its equipment became unstable after a high volume of data flooded its network. The agency said contractor work at one site may be related and that the underlying cause remains under investigation.

Nationwide FAA ground stops are rare. Before the Nov. 2026 shutdown, the last two were after the Sept. 11 attacks and a brief FAA computer outage in January 2023.

#2801. 'We slept here': Flight delays linger but schedule is back on track for Monday

U.S. flights are set to run close to normal on Monday as airlines scramble to get back on track after the Federal Aviation Administration grounded departures nationwide for more than four hours on Friday.

Disruptions lingered on Sunday with thousands more flights canceled and delayed.

Close to 2,000 U.S. flights were canceled as of 4:45 p.m. ET and more than 7,500 were delayed, according to flight-tracking site FlightAware, down from the 6,697 cancellations and 9,000 delays on Saturday, and Friday's 9,201 cancellations and more than 7,400 delays.

American Airlines had the most cancellations on Sunday at 805 flights, about 13% of its schedule, followed by Delta Air Lines with 360 cancellations, or 7% of its schedule, according to aviation data firm Cirium. The airlines' home hubs of Dallas-Fort Worth International Airport and Atlanta Hartsfield-Jackson International Airport, respectively, saw some of the most severe delays in the U.S. Meanwhile, JetBlue Airways canceled close to a third of its flights.

"We slept here," said Beth Martin, who was trying to travel home to Birmingham, Alabama, from New York's LaGuardia Airport on Sunday at the end of her daughter's birthday trip. They were booked for Saturday night, and when delay announcements started rolling in from Delta Air Lines, they decided to make good use of the extra time: after strolling through Central Park, they went window shopping on Fifth Avenue.

But when the flight was canceled, the standby list for a backup flight looked grim, and their original Midtown hotel was charging about $700 for an additional night, the two slept at LaGuardia on Saturday. She said they found wheelchairs to sleep in outside security.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy on Saturday tied the issue to a router and work to update from copper wires, a system overseen by L3Harris Technologies, to a fiber-optic system. FAA administrator Bryan Bedford said the upgrades were part of "routine" maintenance to switch over to fiber optic.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," L3Harris said in a statement Saturday afternoon.

Airlines waived change fees and fare differences and offered pilots and flight attendants extra pay to ease the delays.

Customers told CNBC they were booked on several flights before getting on board.

Justine Fritz, who works in sales for a consumer goods company, was in New York for a business event on Friday. She tried five different flights before getting on a flight to Houston, then flew four hours out of her way to get back to Minneapolis after her Saturday flight was canceled. Fritz said she had so many boarding passes that TSA wouldn't let her through security at first.

"It's been bananas," she told CNBC at the gate. Fritz said she made the best of it by shopping and getting a haircut, but she's still disappointed.

"I'm missing the first hour of the Vikings game," she said.

Martin said her trip home with her daughter will take even longer. Delta booked them to Memphis, and her husband will drive the four hours from Birmingham to pick them up.

Shortly after the groundstop, the longest since 9/11, Duffy pushed Congress for as much as $50 billion for upgrades to the aging U.S. air traffic control system. A day later, Democratic lawmakers pushed back, citing unspent money from existing funding, which includes Congress's approval last year of $12.5 billion for airport and air traffic control improvements over five years.

"Our committee, on behalf of the flying public, will hold the administration accountable for what happened to make sure this chaos does not happen again," said Reps. Rick Larsen (D.-Wash.), ranking member of the House Committee on Transportation and Infrastructure, and Rep. Andre Carson (D.-Ind.), ranking member of the aviation subcommittee, said in a joint statement.

#2799. Germany dominate Serbia in rain-soaked Nations League clash

Germany have made a good start to the last Nations League phase in 2026, beating Serbia 3-1 in a rain-soaked contest. Goals from Wirtz and debutant Gruda mean that Germany still have the chance to top their Nations League group.

The unusual behind-closed-doors atmosphere has seen selected groups of children under 15 admitted alongside teachers and football coaches following UEFA sanctions against Serbia for discrimination. However, the game still delivered on the entertainment front.

Jürgen Klopp's side needed just two minutes to break the deadlock, with Kai Havertz getting on the end of a corner before his attempt was deflected into the net by Srdan Babić for an own goal.

Germany doubled their advantage in the 14th minute following a brilliant, Klopp-esque pressing sequence. Aleksandar Pavlović played a superb through ball to Havertz, whose effort was denied by goalkeeper Vanja Milinković-Savić. Florian Wirtz reacted quickest to the rebound and calmly chipped the ball into the net.

Germany dominated the remainder of the first half, enjoying 75% possession and completing 94% of their passes. Their superiority continued after the break, with Brajan Gruda capping a fantastic solo run with a clinical finish in the 50th minute to make it 3-0.

Serbia responded just six minutes later. Babić made amends for his earlier own goal by reacting quickest after Stefan Gudelj's header from a free kick was saved, converting the rebound to give the hosts a lifeline.

Joshua Kimmich thought he had restored Germany's three-goal advantage shortly afterwards with a powerful low strike from the edge of the box. However, the goal was correctly ruled out due to Kai Havertz's passive offside position.

Derry Scherhant, one of Germany's standout performers under Klopp so far, had enjoyed a relatively quiet evening but came close to scoring in the 70th minute. After cutting inside from the left, his effort beat the goalkeeper but struck the woodwork.

Serbia substitute Aleksandar Mitrović also came close to scoring, but Marc-André ter Stegen stood firm to keep his effort out. Things then went from bad to worse for the hosts as Vanja Dragojevic received a straight red card for a dangerous challenge on Germany captain Joshua Kimmich, accidentally standing on his ankle.

Dragojević was not the only Serbian to leave the pitch early. Substitute Andrija Zivković also saw red after picking up two yellow cards in quick succession following an animated exchange with the referee shortly before full-time.

Klopp used the opportunity to introduce several other Bundesliga names, including Niclas Füllkrug, Felix Nmecha and Said El Mala. However, none managed to make a significant impact during their limited time on the pitch. They could get another opportunity when Germany face the Netherlands on Monday.

The match also marked a historic first for Germany, with Kimmich and Pavlović starting together as a double pivot for the first time. The partnership showed plenty of promise and could become a regular feature under Klopp.

Finally, it was also Germany's last appearance in their long-standing partnership with Adidas. Given the convincing performance, it was certainly a fitting farewell.

#2797. Cavs beat Mavericks 127-117 behind Tyrese Proctor’s triple double

The Cleveland Cavaliers beat the Dallas Mavericks 127-117 behind an inspiring effort from their reserves.

The Cavs played on a skeleton crew tonight, with most of their usual rotation sidelined after last night's delay kept them in Miami longer than expected. Donovan Mitchell, James Harden, Peyton Watson, and Jarrett Allen all sat out while Evan Mobley, Sam Merrill, and Nae'Qwan Tomlin were out with respective injuries.

The starting lineup tonight was Tyrese Proctor, Meleek Thomas, Jaylon Tyson, Mario Hezonja, and Thomas Bryant.

The trio of Proctor, Thomas, and Tyson, who have labeled themselves as 'the pitbulls', set the tone early by being hyper-aggressive and active on both ends of the floor. They combined for 41 points in the first half, doing a little bit of everything to help build an 18-point lead going into the locker room.

Proctor finished the game with 24 points, 11 rebounds, and 10 assists. It was the strongest game of his career and proof of concept that Proctor is a foundational piece of this team's future.

Rocket Arena, which began the night feeling scorned by the stars who sat out, gave a hefty round of applause for the young group that showed up tonight.

Bryant and Hezonja deserve credit as well for serving as the veteran leaders of a younger, more inexperienced lineup. Hezonja scored 22 points on 9-of-12 shooting while Bryant added 19 points on 7-of-12 shooting.

Kyrie Irving scored 34 points and was given a warm reception both when he was announced in the Mavericks' starting lineup and after the Cavs played a video of his iconic Game 7 shot over the Warriors. It was nice to see Irving play basketball back in Cleveland.

Overall, the Cavs' youth showed that you can accomplish a lot by simply playing hard. The cohesion that was shown by this group tonight should be kept in mind moving forward.

#2796. Anthropic’s IPO lands as the embattled AI trade looks for a much-needed bright spot

Between rising interest rates, a nationwide AI backlash, a major political shift in Washington, D.C., and the sudden ouster of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the tech industry is under mounting strain.

Now comes the most important tech IPO in years, and a defining event for the burgeoning but still young artificial intelligence market.

With Anthropic expected to debut on the Nasdaq on Thursday, the stakes are sky-high for almost every major tech company. Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company are collectively investing $26 billion at the IPO price, which is currently expected at $70 to $78 a share. Some of those companies are existing investors, as is early backer Amazon, whose stake could be in the hundreds of billions of dollars.

In addition to all the cash on the line, tech giants also need Anthropic's spending for many years to come. The Claude creator and its top rival, OpenAI, are driving an outsized amount of the AI buildout, and are burning historic amounts of money in the process.

Anthropic has outstanding spending commitments of over $900 billion, according to its latest prospectus. It's on the hook to spend at least $110 billion with Amazon over the course of a decade, and another $400 billion with other companies for cloud capacity, according to filings and partner disclosures. The lab also made $300 billion in commitments with data center and chip partners. Anthropic intends to lay out $125 billion to $150 billion for AI chips from Google, though delivery won't start until 2028.

"The numbers are just staggering," Joe Fath, a partner at venture firm Eclipse and former T. Rowe Price portfolio manager, said in an interview. "It's hard to get your head wrapped around it."

Cloud partners Amazon, Google and Microsoft account for 31% of contractual obligations, Anthropic said in its prospectus. Last year, Amazon built an Indiana data center just for Anthropic, using custom chips.

By going public now, Anthropic can raise a large portion of the money it needs to pay for the technology and infrastructure required for building and hosting its frontier models and expanding portfolio of services. The company recorded a net loss of $104 billion through the first nine months of 2026. The IPO could yield over $90 billion in proceeds, on top of Anthropic's $132 billion in existing cash, including short-term investments.

The length of Anthropic's contracts is also unique. Typically such contracts last five years or less, while Anthropic's agreement with Amazon extends for twice that amount of time.

"Realistically, anything past three to four years, you're basically throwing darts in the dark," said Corey Quinn, chief cloud economist at spend management software vendor Duckbill. "It is almost a certainty that it will be renegotiated long before the culmination of that contract."

Meanwhile, a data center for Anthropic that starts leasing in 2027 has a 20-year term. For both space and computing, Anthropic is splurging now so it doesn't end up short on capacity long into the future.

"In the past, we have significantly underestimated our compute requirements, which left us capacity constrained and impacted both product development and deployment," Anthropic said in the prospectus.

Anthropic and Amazon didn't provide a comment.

It's a tough time for Anthropic to hit the Nasdaq, with the offering coming as tech investors are desperate for some good news. The Nasdaq is down to start November after dropping 5.3% in October, its worst month since March 2025.

Rising interest rates pushed investors out of debt-heavy neoclouds, which have become a critical piece of the infrastructure buildout. And in the midterm elections earlier this month, Democrats won sweeping victories in both houses of Congress and in key gubernatorial races, while dozens of ballot measures were passed that will potentially limit new data center development across the country.

Then came the Nvidia crisis. Shares of the chipmaker at the center of the AI boom tumbled 13% this week to their lowest since July 2025, after Huang's multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, led to his ouster early Thursday. Huang was replaced on an interim basis with CFO Colette Cress, who faces a major test on Tuesday, two days before the Anthropic debut, as Nvidia is set to report quarterly results.

The ongoing Nvidia fallout and the Anthropic IPO create quite the split-screen moment for tech's heavyweights, which are closely tied to both companies.

Google will be paying particularly close attention.

The company is set to own about 13% of Anthropic after the IPO, and is backstopping the lab's $19 billion in lease commitments to Terawulf, which will supply data center space in Kentucky. If Anthropic goes insolvent, Google will step in and pay the remaining rent or a termination fee, Terawulf said.

"Everybody knows that Anthropic is going to be the future of Google," said Subhadeep Chatterjee, a former Google cloud partnerships leader who now advises companies.

One major impetus for Anthropic to enter costly and long-term guarantees is that OpenAI is being equally aggressive, and capacity is limited, particularly for graphics processing units. Going long can mean locking in rates as the industry confronts questions about whether data center development will slow.

"You will need to be in a rush to get infrastructure, and if you are not going to be there, you will be out of business," said Eli Mansoor, CEO of OskaQ Consulting, which advises companies on cloud negotiations. "It used to be that prices always go down, but this is changing, especially in the GPU world."

Big financial firms are lining up to help Anthropic get what it wants. Bank of America, Blackstone, Citigroup and Morgan Stanley have offered $63 billion to buy racks full of Broadcom AI chips that Anthropic will rent. Broadcom has provided a backstop with a maximum liability of $43 billion.

That's a lot of silicon and a massive bet for a five-year-old company that's never lived through a technology downturn.

"It's not a risk-free prospect, but I would call it smart investing," said David Tollen, founder of Sycamore Legal, where he specializes in IT contracts. "They're investing, in a sense, in themselves. They're putting money into something that if it succeeds, they get a piece of the action."

#2795. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

#2792. Juice your growth and save on taxes in this workplace investment account

Workers are in the heart of open enrollment season for next year's benefits, and rising healthcare costs are shining a spotlight on an offering that includes a tax-advantaged investment account.

Healthcare expenses are climbing for workers and employers alike, with average total family premiums, including the employer's share, averaging $28,303, up 5% from a year ago, according to data from health policy organization KFF, formerly the Kaiser Family Foundation.

To manage the costs, a growing number of employers are moving to high-deductible health plans, which work alongside health savings accounts or HSAs. Almost 40% of companies with at least 10 employees that offer health benefits also have an HSA-qualified plan, KFF found.

Health savings accounts have three major tax advantages. First, contributions that your employer automatically deducts from your pay aren't taxed. Second, money stashed into the HSA grows free of taxes. Finally, you can make tax-free withdrawals for qualified health expenses – just be sure to save your receipts.

"I love HSAs because of their triple-tax advantage," said Eileen Olson, CFP and founder of Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina, near Savannah, Georgia. "For people who can afford to leave it alone, it can be one of the best retirement accounts they're going to have."

In 2027, those with individual healthcare coverage through a high-deductible plan can stash up to $4,500 in an HSA, including any contributions from employers. That number goes up to $9,000 for family plans. Individuals can kick in a catch-up contribution of $1,000 if they're aged 55 and older and not enrolled in Medicare.

The HSA differs from the healthcare flexible spending account, known as the FSA.

FSA funds must be used by the end of the plan year unless your employer allows a carryover of up to $700 or an additional 2.5 months to use the money. HSAs, however, have no expiration date on their funds – and that's what makes them such a powerful tool for long-term planning. You can also take the HSA with you if you change employers.

"Get the money deployed, take advantage of the pretax contribution, and allocate the money into a growth portfolio," said Mark Stancato, certified financial planner at VIP Wealth Advisors in Decatur, Georgia. "With the HSA, you can keep compounding year over year."

The structure of an HSA and the available investment menu will vary from one plan to the next, and some will require that employees keep some of their holdings in cash.

Preset HSA fund menus tend to have 15 to 25 funds to choose from, and providers say that accountholders who invest tend to use two to four funds, according to Greg Carlson, senior research analyst for equity strategies at Morningstar.

How you allocate your HSA will depend on several factors, including your family's health prospects in the next year, your cash flow needs – how much you can comfortably sock into the HSA – and your risk tolerance, said André Small, CFP and founder of A Small Investment in Houston.

Small's standard is to keep an amount equal to 120% of the plan's out-of-pocket maximum held in cash in the HSA.

"That covers a bad year without forcing a sale at the wrong time," he said. "Above that amount? I typically advise investing the amount the client is comfortable investing, allocated to their risk tolerance, their time horizon and the goal of spending these dollars on care later."

For 2027, the IRS defines a high-deductible plan as one with an annual deductible no less than $1,750 for self-only coverage ($3,500 for family plans) and with an out-of-pocket expense limit that doesn't exceed $8,700 for self-only (or $17,400 for family coverage). Out-of-pocket expenses include deductibles, copays and other costs – but not premiums.

Other advisors suggest having up to the deductible in cash, though some HSA plan providers require a stated minimum amount in cash.

Beyond this cash reserve, financial advisors recommend allocating into stocks.

Patrick Swift, CFP and president of Amplius Wealth Advisors in Blue Bell, Pennsylvania, aims for an allocation of up to 60% in U.S. large cap stocks, 20% in small and mid-cap stocks and 20% in international stocks.

"We believe the equity portfolio should be well diversified," he said. "If the [HSA] balance is 2% to 10% of your net worth, why not be aggressive? That can change based on the situation, but if you are comfortable with the philosophical aspect, why not be growth oriented?"

Similarly, Stancato likes to go heavy on equity exposure in HSA accounts. "I allocate more dollars into small caps and emerging markets – those asset classes with more volatility but also more opportunity for growth," the advisor said. He also adds some large-cap domestic exposure.

For the adventurous, select HSA providers are offering brokerage window access, meaning investors can pick from a broader array of investments, including individual stocks.

This year, seven providers surveyed by Morningstar offer access to brokerage windows – up from four in 2023 – including HealthEquity, Fidelity and Lively. Be aware that there may be annual fees attached, either a flat dollar amount or a percentage of assets.

Rachel Elson, a CFP and wealth advisor at Perigon Wealth Management in San Francisco, recommends keeping things simple. "You can do a three-ETF model, covering some allocation that includes the U.S. market, and two ETFs for developed and emerging markets."

"You don't need to get super complicated here," she added.

#2791. Bessent calls for ‘dramatic shakeup’ at Nvidia, urges outside CEO

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent on Sunday called for a "dramatic shakeup" at Nvidia after CEO Jensen Huang's resignation, questioning whether the chipmaker pursued illicit sales of its advanced artificial intelligence chips to boost earnings.

Bessent said Nvidia's board should look outside the company for its next chief executive and suggested other executives should face scrutiny over the chip giant's compliance with U.S. export controls.

"I want to keep coming back to what was the culture at Nvidia, and were they pressing these seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings?" Bessent said in an interview on Fox News.

The remarks come amid a growing feud between Bessent and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick over the suspected diversion of advanced AI chips to China, even as the Treasury secretary continues to defend President Donald Trump's handling of the matter.

Both Bessent and Lutnick are expected to accompany Trump to China this week, Semafor reported Sunday, putting the two Cabinet secretaries alongside the president as he prepares to meet with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin on the sidelines of the APEC summit.

A White House official told Reuters on Thursday that the president maintains "complete confidence" in Lutnick, despite mounting scrutiny of the secretary’s handling of the Nvidia matter.

Huang resigned Thursday following revelations about his relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed, whom U.S. intelligence officials suspect of spying for China. Megaspeed is suspected of diverting Nvidia chips to China in violation of U.S. export controls.

Nvidia named Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress interim CEO.

Bessent suggested Sunday that Nvidia's problems extended well beyond Huang.

"If this were a bank and they hadn't complied like this, the OCC would be in there right now, and a lot of the executives would be removed," Bessent said, referring to the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

Bessent also questioned Nvidia's financial disclosures, pointing to a surge in reported Singapore revenue as its China sales fell.

Nvidia stopped separately reporting Singapore revenue in November 2025, saying a new methodology based on customers' headquarters rather than billing addresses better reflected the geographic distribution of its sales.

Bessent's remarks came two days after he told CNBC's "Squawk Box" that several federal agencies had supported adding Megaspeed to the government's export blacklist for months.

"But Commerce has the pen," Bessent said.

The public rebuke followed reporting by Axios that Bessent confronted Lutnick in the White House Situation Room over Commerce's failure to act against Megaspeed. Commerce denied that the confrontation took place.

Lutnick is also facing scrutiny over his family's financial ties to Nvidia. CNBC reported Friday that Cantor Fitzgerald, the Wall Street firm formerly run by Lutnick and now led by his sons, has helped arrange billions of dollars in financing for Nvidia's business partners.

Cantor said Nvidia share holdings fell nearly 50% over six months as national security concerns surrounding Huang escalated. The positions were associated with client swap transactions, not proprietary investments, the investment bank said.

There is no evidence Lutnick shared sensitive information with his sons or that Cantor traded on such information.

Bessent on Sunday said Trump had drawn a firm line against sales of chips more advanced than Nvidia's H200 to China. Trump himself authorized H200 sales to approved Chinese customers in December —following months of lobbying by Huang — in an arrangement under which the U.S. government would receive a 25% cut.

Bessent's latest criticism comes two days before Nvidia is scheduled to report its first quarterly earnings since Huang's departure.

#2790. The Knicks are struggling to finish games to start the season

It was just a few months ago that you could've turned a Knicks game off and celebrated within the last five minutes if it was a one-possession game: that's how automatic they were in the clutch.

Led by the former Clutch Player of the Year and an impeccable supporting cast, it seemed like every time they found themselves in a late and close situation, it was automatic after those two CJ McCollum games. Even going back further than that, this team's ability to dig deep when they need it most has been the defining characteristic of the Jalen Brunson era.

With an identical roster back in 2026-27, you'd expect it'd be the same… except it's not.

In the Knicks' 6-6 start, they've played seven clutch games. They've lost six of them.

Not all clutch games are created equal, but all of these games were winnable. Even last night's loss against Indiana, which I was saying felt like a "fake clutch game," as the Knicks got a few buckets late to make it look close, they somehow managed to have the ball with a chance to send it to overtime at the buzzer.

One way or another, the Knicks have failed to win these games. The lone win, the high-scoring thriller in Chicago, was the only game where it's felt like last season's Knicks that found a way.

Overall, in their 1-6 record in clutch games, the Knicks have a minus-17.4 net rating, ranking 22nd in the NBA. They don't do anything truly bad in these games, but they do nothing well.

Their turnover rate is league average, as is their assist rate. Their rebounding goes from dominant to so-so, but the real culprit is the putrid 50.7 TS%, which ranks 23rd. That's the catalyst behind an offense that hasn't worked in these situations.

If you exclude the Chicago game, they have a minus-40 net rating with an 82.9 offensive rating and an even rougher 44.4 TS%. All six of those games have come against the type of teams they've excelled against in postseason settings, but it just hasn't happened so far.

Going game-by-game, it's just a mess of missed opportunities.

In the season opener against Philly, the Knicks trailed by two with under three minutes to go. After giving up a dunk to VJ Edgecombe, OG Anunoby committed a back-breaking turnover in the open court that allowed Tyrese Maxey to score an easy layup. The Knicks never got closer.

The Boston game that followed it felt like a fake comeback at times, but the team got within one with 1:41 to go. While the Celtics never pulled away from there, Jayson Tatum never gave them a chance to come back with tough shot after tough shot.

The inability to get a stop, coupled with a bad moving screen by Karl-Anthony Towns in a crucial spot down three with 1:11 left doomed them to an 0-2 start.

Against Orlando, the game was tied at 91 at the five-minute mark, but the Knicks played so poorly over the next four minutes that they gave themselves no chance. On their next eight possessions, the Knicks went 0-for-3 from the field, 2-for-6 from the line, and had three turnovers.

In the NBA Cup game in Philadelphia, a stepback three from Brunson gave them a 90-89 lead with 1:32 left, but things would unravel after that. A clutch LeBron fadeaway and a triple from Edgecombe on the next two possessions hurt, but so did allowing a brutal offensive rebound after getting a stop with 12 seconds left.

Their biggest strength, rebounding, failed them.

Against Cleveland on Wednesday, it was tied entering clutch time and a one-point game with 3:26 left, but a peculiar decision to continue to blitz Donovan Mitchell at halfcourt allowed the Cavs to score on essentially every trip down.

One sequence that defined this one? Deuce McBride had a fastbreak layup down by five with 35 seconds to go, got stuffed, and Peyton Watson hit Jarrett Allen on an outlet for an and-1 to ice the game.

Then, last night in Indiana, the undermanned Pacers did everything they could to throw the game away, giving the Knicks the ball down by two with 2.8 to go. They drew up a post touch for Towns, but it didn't go.

I don't hate the idea, but I really wish they used Towns' newfound playmaking ability to their advantage by trying to free up a shooter on the right wing. If I have any criticism of this team's late-game offense for years now, it's too much isolation. There was enough time for a pass to at least be an option.

The team is shooting 38.9% in the clutch, a continuation of what's been annoyingly persistent struggles with efficiency. They haven't shot enough free throws to make conclusions there, but the regression we've seen from Towns and Brunson at the line has killed them in games where every point counts.

All told, this is something you expect to eventually reverse itself, but we're getting to the point in the season where samples are getting bigger and bigger.

At some point, they have to start executing in general and consistently play as a cohesive unit for all 48 minutes.

#2788. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long term

Major indexes were volatile this week due to earnings from artificial intelligence companies, key economic releases, and the scandal involving Nvidia former CEO Jensen Huang. While short-term fluctuations could weigh on investor sentiment, it is prudent to maintain a long-term investment horizon.

Investors looking for attractive long-term picks can track the ratings of top Wall Street analysts and gain key insights from their in-depth research on the macro and micro factors impacting stocks.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Cybersecurity company Palo Alto Networks will announce its fiscal first-quarter earnings on Nov. 18. Migration to the cloud and the rapid adoption of AI have increased demand for cybersecurity solutions provided by Palo Alto and its peers. TipRanks' KPIs tool for PANW shows the rise in the company's subscription revenue over the years, driven by the adoption of its AI and security offerings and contributions from recent acquisitions.

Ahead of the upcoming earnings, Oppenheimer analyst Ittai Kidron reiterated a buy rating on Palo Alto stock with a price target of $450. The five-star analyst said that his checks indicate upside potential from CyberArk and Chronosphere acquisitions and strength in the company's core businesses.

Specifically, Kidron expects Palo Alto's Q1 FY27 next-generation security annualized recurring revenue to be about two percentage points above the midpoint of the guidance range and consensus, or $9.55 billion. He also sees nearly 1 to 1.5 percentage points of revenue upside compared to the guidance and consensus of $3.305 billion. Kidron expects management to remain conservative with its FY27 guidance and raise it by no more than the size of the quarterly beat.

Additionally, Kidron highlighted that partner checks indicated above-plan performance in the quarter, driven by firewall-attached subscriptions and strength in Prisma SASE and XSIAM offerings. The analyst also expects results to gain from CyberArk cross-selling and better-than-expected performance of Chronosphere as OpenAI completes its migration.

"While Prisma AIRS crossed $100M in ARR last quarter, the fastest product ramp in company history, partners still view CY27 as the first real revenue year," said Kidron.

Kidron ranks No. 638 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.5%. See Palo Alto Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

Next on this week's list is database software company MongoDB. Recently, BTIG analyst Gray Powell initiated coverage of MDB stock with a buy rating and a price target of $417. Powell estimates that currently the size of the company's core addressable market is nearly $15 billion, with several tailwinds to drive more than 20% revenue growth over the next few years.

Beyond its core document and vector businesses, Powell highlighted that MongoDB has exposure to about $72 billion of adjacent markets, projected to grow to about $121 billion over four years. With even modest share gains in adjacent markets, the five-star analyst believes that MongoDB can grow revenue at about a 24% annual rate over the next three years, above management's growth target of more than 20%.

Contrary to the bearish narrative, Powell views AI as an emerging tailwind for MongoDB, with application modernization creating new workloads while making migrations faster and cheaper. He added that interviews with eight contacts were generally constructive and indicated faster legacy migrations, growing non-relational adoption, and early traction in Voyage AI and Vector Search offerings.

Additionally, Powell sees upside to Atlas growth, driven by new products. "We see a meaningful product cycle led by MongoDB 9.0 and AMP, with Agent Engine and Atlas Infinite adding upside over time," said the analyst.

Powell ranks No. 480 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13%. See MongoDB Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

Membership-only warehouse chain Costco Wholesale recently announced a special cash dividend of $30 per share, payable on Dec. 4. The company also announced that its board has reauthorized a stock repurchase program of up to $4 billion, which will expire in November 2030 and replaces the current $4 billion program that is set to end in January 2027.

Following the announcement, Oppenheimer analyst Rupesh Parikh reiterated a buy rating on COST stock with a price target of $1,160. The five-star analyst said that the special dividend news is one of the key catalysts that he has been waiting for since Costco was added to Oppenheimer's top pick list in November 2025.

Parikh highlighted that the special dividend the company announced is significantly above his estimate of about $20 per share. The analyst expects the special dividend to reduce interest income substantially.

"COST remains a top pick for us, and we still see the potential for a stock split down the road, which could represent another positive catalyst for shares, in our view," said Parikh.

Overall, Parikh remains bullish on Costco's long-term prospects, backed by its value proposition, leading competitive position, and a solid track record of shareholder returns. The analyst also noted the company's strong management team and the ability to sustain top- and bottom-line growth even in a competitive retail backdrop.

Parikh ranks No. 324 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.7%. See Costco Statistics on TipRanks.

#2785. Cavaliers backups beat Mavericks as all 5 Cleveland starters sit out Saturday night's win

The Cleveland Cavaliers beat the Dallas Mavericks 127-117 on Saturday night.

While the win itself is notable — Cleveland entered the game at 6-7 and Dallas at 7-5 — the story was the players the Cavaliers won with.

Saturday night was Cleveland's second game in a back-to-back. The Cavs were in Miami on Friday night and lost 133-121. With the nationwide domestic flight ground stop lasting four hours on Friday, travel plans across sports leagues were impacted.

Cleveland's game finished after the ground stop ended, but the decision had already been made for the Cavaliers to fly back home Saturday morning. The team landed in Cleveland around noon local time, and then had a quick turnaround for Saturday night's game.

The Cavaliers ruled out all five of their usual starters. James Harden, Donovan Mitchell, Peyton Watson, Evan Mobley and Jarrett Allen were inactive ahead of the game.

This led Cleveland to start Mario Hezonja, Thomas Bryant, Jaylon Tyson, Tyrese Proctor and Meleek Thomas against a full-strength Mavericks squad.

Proctor led the way with a triple-double. He finished with 24 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists. Hezonja added 22 points, while Bryant had 19 points and 11 boards.

Cleveland's bench was shallow with only three players used, but they had an impact. Not only did the three eat up necessary minutes, but Norchad Omier had 13 points and eight rebounds, while Craig Porter Jr. added 9 points, 9 rebounds and 6 assists.

Dallas' 10-point loss against the backups didn't bring many positives, but six players finished with double-digit points, including all five starters. Kyrie Irving paced the team with 34 points, while star forward Cooper Flagg had 18 points, 8 rebounds and 6 assists.

Cleveland now gets a much-needed day off and returns to action Monday against the Chicago Bulls. Dallas has its own back-to-back to deal with and will end its three-game road trip on Sunday against the Boston Celtics.

#2783. Classic Cousins, Dominant Defense Lead Way as Vikings Thrash Packers

For the first eight games of their 2026 season, the Minnesota Vikings' offense struggled to put points on the board while their defense made life miserable for offenses around the NFL. On Sunday afternoon at Lambeau Field, it appears that the tide might be turning for the Minnesota offense, thanks to the addition of one man.

Kirk Cousins threw a pair of touchdown passes and, more importantly, allowed the Minnesota defense to actually get some rest after two straight weeks of getting overworked. The defense responded by sacking Jordan Love eight times and shutting down an offense that had scored 45 points the previous week in a thoroughly dominant 30-10 win over the Green Bay Packers in Week 10 action at Lambeau Field.

The Packers won the opening coin toss and deferred to the second half, putting Kirk Cousins and the Minnesota offense on the field first. The Vikings went three-and-out, as Cousins' first pass was too high for Justin Jefferson and his second, a screen to Myles Price, was dropped for a loss of yards. Brett Thorson blasted a 64-yard punt, and the Packers started their first possession inside their own 10-yard line.

On the Packers' second play from scrimmage, Andrew Van Ginkel made his presence felt, as Jordan Love completed a pass to tight end Mark Redman, and Van Ginkel stripped it away, recovering the fumble at the Green Bay 10 for a turnover.

The Vikings' offense capitalized on the turnover with. . .a false start and a sack of Cousins that moved them back 14 yards in two plays. After an Aaron Jones run on third down, the Vikings called on Will Reichard, who connected on a 34-yard field goal to give the Vikings a 3-0 lead just under five minutes into the contest.

Green Bay got some help on the ensuing kickoff, as Jay Ward committed a 15-yard penalty to put the Packers near midfield to start their next possession. The Packers moved the ball into Minnesota territory inside the 30-yard line, but the drive came to a screeching halt when Dallas Turner dropped Love for a sack. The Packers brought on Trey Smack to attempt a 53-yard field goal, but his attempt sailed wide to the right to give Minnesota the ball back near midfield, still leading 3-0.

Minnesota started their next drive with a first down into Green Bay territory, as Cousins found Gavin Bartholomew to move the chains. The Vikings then got a look at a familiar connection, as Cousins found Justin Jefferson for a 13-yard gain to convert on third down and keep the drive moving. The Vikings were then called for a face mask penalty to push themselves back to the edge of field goal range. . .but Cousins decided that the Vikings didn't need a field goal, as he hit Myles Price for a 38-yard touchdown that saw Price bounce off of a Green Bay defender and make his way into the end zone.

The first receiving touchdown of Price's NFL career increased the Minnesota lead to 10-0 with just over two minutes left in the first quarter. And in the ninth game of the season, that was the Vikings' first offensive touchdown in the first quarter of any game this season.

Green Bay got off to a quick start on their next drive, as Love found Marshawn Lloyd on a quick pass for a big gain to near midfield, and Lloyd then took a handoff into Minnesota territory for another first down. The Vikings then got called for a couple of 15-yard penalties, one on Eric Wilson for roughing the passer and another on Dallas Turner for. . .something. . .to help move the Packers into the red zone. But when it looked like the Packers might get on the scoreboard, Harrison Smith decided to put an end to all that.

The Hitman picked off Jordan Love in the end zone to end the drive and bring the ball out to the 20-yard line for the Vikings' next possession. Unfortunately, the Vikings' offense couldn't do anything with it, as a loss on first down stalled the drive early as they went three-and-out again. Thorson's punt put the Green Bay offense at its own 25 to start their next possession.

The Vikings' defense went to work again, as they registered back-to-back sacks of Love to force a three-and-out, one from Levi Drake Rodriguez and one from Eric Wilson. Green Bay's punt gave the Vikings good field position at their own 45-yard line to start their next possession. The Vikings marched the ball down the field with a nice mix of passes before a run by Jordan Mason put the ball into the red zone.

The Vikings then dodged a bullet when it looked like Cousins had thrown an interception, but it was ruled by replay that his knee was down before he threw the ball, allowing the Vikings to keep possession. Cousins then hit Tai Felton on a big third-down throw to make it first-and-goal. On the next play, the former Packer, Aaron Jones, took a pitch and went around the left side for a 2-yard score to extend the Minnesota lead to 17-0 with just over six minutes left in the first half.

Green Bay's offense went three-and-out on its next possession after a third-down catch by Matthew Golden that had been ruled a first-down completion was ruled incomplete after Kevin O'Connell challenged the call, as replay showed that the ball hit the ground before Golden controlled it. Minnesota took over again and pushed into Green Bay territory before the two-minute warning, but on the first snap after the two-minute timeout, Cousins' pass for Tai Felton was overthrown and intercepted by Xavier McKinney for a turnover.

The Packers took the ball after the interception and got the ball close to midfield, but then the Vikings' defense turned things up again and collected two more sacks of Love: one that was split between Van Ginkel and Kyle Van Noy, and one that Van Noy got all by himself. That concluded a five-sack first half for the Vikings, and they went into the locker room with a 17-0 lead.

Green Bay got the ball to start the second half, and pushed their first possession to near midfield. However, they turned the ball over again when a shotgun snap flew right past Jordan Love for a fumble that Dallas Turner recovered at the Green Bay 27-yard line for the third turnover of the game for Green Bay, setting the Vikings up nicely.

The Vikings got the ball into a goal-to-go situation, but the offense stalled, and they had to settle for another field goal from Reichard, this one a 32-yarder to make it 20-0 with ten minutes left in the third quarter.

The Packers got the ball back and once again moved into Minnesota territory, but the sack parade continued again, with Jalen Redmond and Elijah Williams taking Love down to add to the total. The Packers faced a 4th-and-11 from the Minnesota 44-yard line, and they converted on a pass from Love to Matthew Golden to stay on the field. On the next play, Green Bay finally got on the scoreboard, as Love found J. Michael Sturdivant for a 32-yard score to make it 20-7 with just over five minutes remaining in the third quarter of play.

Minnesota went three-and-out on its next possession, putting Green Bay at their 14-yard line to start their next drive. Love and company went back to work and started to heat up, working the middle of the field with passes to Christian Watson and Tucker Kraft to push the ball towards the red zone. But their drive stalled out, and they settled for a 40-yard field goal attempt from Smack, who put it between the uprights to make the score 20-10 with just 19 seconds left in the third quarter.

The Vikings took over on offense and put together one of their best offensive drives of the season, mixing runs and passes while getting a few key third-down conversions, pushing the ball into the red zone and eating six and a half minutes off the clock. The drive ended with the first NFL touchdown in the career of tight end Gavin Bartholomew, who took a pass from Cousins and turned it into a 20-yard score to put the Vikings up 27-10 with eight and a half minutes remaining in regulation.

On the ensuing kickoff, the Packers gave up turnover number four, as Jakobe Thomas. . .who was active for the first time in his NFL career today after giving his #8 to Kirk Cousins. . .forced a fumble on the return from Sturdivant, and the ball was recovered by Jake Golday at the Green Bay 35-yard line.

Minnesota got the ball down to the 2-yard line, but wound up giving Will Reichard another field goal attempt, a 20-yarder that split the uprights to extend the Vikings' lead to 30-10 with just under four minutes remaining in regulation.

The Packers officially raised the white flag on their next drive, taking Love out of the game and putting Tyrod Taylor into the game at quarterback. For the afternoon, Love was officially sacked eight times by Minnesota defenders, the most by a Vikings team ever in a game at Lambeau Field and two off of the franchise record for the most sacks in a game.

Cousins was solid, if not spectacular, which is really all the Vikings need at this point. He completed 21-of-31 passes for 207 yards, a pair of touchdown passes, and an interception. More importantly, the Vikings held on to the ball for over thirty-five minutes after two weeks where their opponents were able to keep the Minnesota defense on the field for extended periods of time and wear them down

The victory snapped Minnesota's two-game losing streak and put them into a three-way tie atop the NFC North with the Chicago Bears, who had Week 10 off, and the Detroit Lions, who thumped the New England Patriots in Munich, Germany, earlier in the day. All three teams have 7-2 records. It won't get any easier for the Vikings next week, as they'll make a trip to Mexico City to face another of the NFL's best teams, the San Francisco 49ers, on Sunday Night Football.

The Vikings sweep the 2026 Border Battle from the Green Bay Packers, taking it home with a thoroughly convincing 30-10 win at Lambeau Field in Week 10. Thank you to everybody who got their coverage of this week's game right here at The Daily Norseman!

#2778. CeeDee Lamb is 96 yards from becoming first Cowboys' player with six straight 1,000-yard seasons

It may not happen today, but it’ll happen at some point.

Cowboys receiver CeeDee Lamb, with only 96 receiving yards over the next eight games, will become the first player in franchise history with six straight 1,000-yard receiving seasons.

It’s an amazing stat, given the great receivers the Cowboys have had — starting with those who, like Lamb, have worn No. 88: Dez Bryant, Michael Irvin, and Drew Pearson, most notably. Tight end Jason Witten had four in six years, with 952 and 942 peppered between them.

For the season, Lamb has a league-high 108 targets. He has made 70 catches for 904 yards and seven touchdowns.

More importantly, the 4-5 Cowboys have an opportunity to gain ground on the 5-3-1 Giants atop the NFC East. It won’t be easy against the 7-1 49ers. Especially since quarterback Dak Prescott has lost his last four starts against the 49ers.

The Dallas Mavericks (7-6) fell to the Cleveland Cavaliers (7-7), 127-117 in a Saturday night matchup on the road. The matchup followed Dallas' 133-94 beatdown loss to the Minnesota Timberwolves on Thursday night. The Mavericks needed a bounce-back performance after Thursday night's blunder, and failed to take advantage of a second-string Cavaliers team.

Max Christie, Sergio de Larrea, Dereck Lively, and Tarik Biberovic were out for the Mavericks. Cleveland was without Donovan Mitchell, James Harden, Peyton Watson, Evan Mobley, Jarret Allen, and Sam Merrill. The Cavaliers sat all their top players after being unable to leave Miami until 9 a.m. due to a nationwide FAA grounding, following their 133-121 loss to the Heat. The nationwide grounding led to a call from Mitchell to cancel the game.

Kyrie Irving led the Mavericks with 34 points, six rebounds, and five assists, with 28 of those points coming in the second half. Tyrese Proctor contributed 24 points, 11 rebounds, and 10 assists, his first career triple-double, for the shorthanded Cavaliers.

The Cavaliers, though shorthanded, had a hot hand early, allowing them to compete with the Mavericks in the first period. The Cavaliers' early hot hand from behind the arc faded away in the second quarter, but they were able to go inside, as the Mavericks continued to struggle to defend them. The Mavericks needed more from Cooper Flagg in this quarter, who went 1-of-6 from the field as the Cavaliers' lead grew behind their 28-9 run in the quarter. Dallas walked into the intermission after giving up 38 points in the period, down 71-53, with a desperate need to punch back in the second half.

Dallas entered the second half uninspired to turn around its double-digit deficit, letting the lead swell to over twenty points. Dallas attempted to make a run midway through the third period, cutting the lead to 14, but continued to be unable to defend Cleveland to close the lead in any meaningful way. After a string of defensive stops, the Mavericks closed the gap with the Cavaliers, cutting the lead to 10. Irving provided a necessary 20 points in the third quarter when the Mavericks couldn't find a bucket. Even then, the Mavericks entered the fourth quarter with a 14-point deficit to dig themselves out of. The Mavericks' struggles seeped into the fourth quarter, despite everything Irving tried to do for Dallas. The Mavericks were unable to string together enough stops to fight back, falling 127-117.

Here are three takeaways from Saturday night's loss.

The Mavericks' offensive struggles continued on the road, shooting 39-of-94 from the field in the loss to Cleveland. Dallas needed someone to step up alongside Irving, but failed to take advantage of a less-talented group. This team needs more from guys like PJ Washington and Naji Marshall, who both shot poorly from the field in the loss to the Cavaliers. Off-nights from Flagg mean that someone else needs to answer the call, and the Mavericks failed to find someone to do so against Cleveland.

Cooper Flagg struggled in Saturday night's matchup with the Cavaliers, finishing with 18 points on 6-of-19 from the field. He was unable to find his shot tonight, and it felt like he was trying to force himself out of his slump by trying to draw whistles that he didn't get. He shot 0-of-6 from behind the arc tonight, though the Mavericks shot poorly from three as a whole. The Mavericks need more from Flagg, especially when facing a team missing its best players, because Irving can't be left to do it on his own. Flagg's youth showed tonight, and the Mavericks will need him to return to his All-Star-caliber self.

Kyrie Irving's 20-point third quarter gave the Mavericks a glimmer of temporary hope, with the Mavericks closing the Cavaliers' lead to 10. He was able to find his shot in this quarter, giving the Mavericks the push they needed to try to close the gap on the Cavaliers. Irving finished with 34 points, with the Mavericks needing every last one. Irving has played like an All-Star since returning from his ACL tear, and tonight was another impressive showing, coming when Dallas needed it the most. The Mavericks were unable to find anyone to step up to the plate alongside Irving and play with some shame against the Cleveland B-team.
